<meta name="citation_language" content="English">
<meta name="citation_title" content="Genetics and Breeding of Lupinus mutabilis: An Emerging Protein Crop">
<meta name="citation_keywords" content="Lupinus mutabilis; Lupin; Breeding; Genetics; Protein crop; Plant protein">
<meta name="citation_abstract" content="Protein crops have gained increasing interest in recent years, as a transition towards plant-protein based diets appears pivotal to ensure global food security and preserve the environment. The Andean species Lupinus mutabilis emerges as an ideal protein crop with great potential for Europe and other regions with temperate climates. This species is characterized by an oil and protein content similar to soybean and is highly valued for its adaptability to colder climates and low input agriculture on marginal land. However, its introduction outside the Andes has yet to take off. To date, L. mutabilis remains an under-studied crop, lacking high yield, early maturity and a consistent breeding history. This review paper identifies L. mutabilis limitations and potential uses,  and suggests the main breeding targets for further improvement of this crop. It also highlights the potential of new molecular tools and available germplasm resources that can now be used to establish L. mutabilis as a viable protein crop.">

class="Avatar Avatar--size-40 Avatar--text Avatar--grey PeopleListItem__Avatar"><!--[--><span class="notranslate">L</span><span class="notranslate">M</span><!--]--></div><p class="PeopleListItem__name notranslate">Luisa M. Trindade  <sup>1</sup><sup>*</sup><!----></p></a></li><!----><li class="PeopleList__spacer" aria-hidden="true"></li></ul><div class="AffiliationList"><ul class="AffiliationList__list"><!--[--><li class="AffiliationList__item"><p class="AffiliationList__name notranslate"><span>1.</span> Wageningen University &amp; Research Plant Breeding, Wageningen University, Wageningen, Netherlands</p></li><li class="AffiliationList__item"><p class="AffiliationList__name notranslate"><span>2.</span> DRAT, Instituto Superior de Agronomia, Universidade de Lisboa, Tapada da Ajuda, Lisbon, Portugal</p></li><!--]--><!----></ul></div></div><div style="display:none;" class="ArticleMetrics"><p class="ArticleMetrics__title"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 48 48" fill="currentColor" width="16px" style="vertical-align:middle;display:inline-block;height:auto;"><path d="M39.798 40.512a1.227 1.227 0 0 0 1.2-1.233v-1.434a1.22 1.22 0 0 0-1.2-1.224H37.02l.024-24.402a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2-1.2h-6.93a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2 1.2v24.45h-3.732V21.54a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2-1.2h-6.98a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2 1.2v15.117h-3.778V8.7a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2-1.2H8.19a1.2 1.2 0 0 0-1.2 1.2v30.615a1.2 1.2 0 0 0 1.2 1.2l31.608-.003Zm-21.444-3.9V24.105h1.89V36.6l-1.89.012Zm13.098 0V14.82h1.866v21.792h-1.866Z"></path></svg> Article metrics </p><a class="ArticleMetrics__link" href="#metrics" data-event="articleMetrics-a-viewDetails">View details</a><!----></div><!----><div class="ArticleDetailsV4__main__content"><div class="ArticleContent"><!--[--><!--]--><div id="h1"><h2>Abstract</h2><!--[--><p>Protein crops have gained increasing interest in recent years, as a transition towards plant-protein based diets appears pivotal to ensure global food security and preserve the environment. The Andean species <!--[--><i>Lupinus mutabilis</i><!--]--> emerges as an ideal protein crop with great potential for Europe and other regions with temperate climates. This species is characterized by oil and protein content similar to soybean and is highly valued for its adaptability to colder climates and low input agriculture on marginal land. However, its introduction outside the Andes has yet to take off. To date, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> remains an under-studied crop, lacking high yield, early maturity and a consistent breeding history. This review paper identifies <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> limitations and potential uses, and suggests the main breeding targets for further improvement of this crop. It also highlights the potential of new molecular tools and available germplasm resources that can now be used to establish <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as a viable protein crop.</p><!--]--></div><!----><!--[--><div id="s1"><!--[--><h2>Introduction</h2><p>Over the past decades, challenges such as food security and environmental sustainability have earned the status of main priorities worldwide and are the basis of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) defined by the United Nations in 2015. As world population continues to rise, our food production has already exceeded the planet’s environmental boundaries driving climate changes, biodiversity loss and unsustainable use of land and water. The growing demand for animal proteins has played an important role in this process, by turning livestock sector in the main user of agricultural land and in one of the biggest contributors to climate change. In light of the current situation, a transition from meat-intensive diets towards plant proteins-based diets is vital to ensure global food security and preserve the environment.</p><p>To create alternatives to animal protein, the cultivation of protein crops has gained interest in recent years. The European Union has launched initiatives to reduce its dependency on the import of soybean by growing an increasing quantity and variety of protein crops within the European member states. Research has focused on identifying sources of proteins that can reduce the current protein deficit while contributing to the transition to more sustainable agricultural systems. One such source is protein-rich leguminous plants. Legumes also stand out for their great potential in the reclamation of poor and marginal lands for agriculture, due to their ability to fix nitrogen and their beneficial effects on the soil (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B26-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b26"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->De Ron et al., 2017<!--]--></button>). Among legumes, lupins have been identified as particularly promising, characterized by high-quality protein content, suitability for sustainable production and potential health benefits (<a href="#B60" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b60"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Lucas et al., 2015<!--]--></a>). The genus Lupinus includes almost 300 species, but only four play an important role in agriculture: <!--[--><i>L. albus</i><!--]-->, <!--[--><i>L. angustifolius</i><!--]-->, <!--[--><i>L. luteus</i><!--]--> and <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B39-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b39"><!--[--><span id="B39a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gresta et al., 2017<!--]--></button>). The first three listed species are native to Europe and represent the majority of lupins cultivated worldwide. At the same time, despite years of extensive research, the success of these species has been hampered by unstable yields, low oil content and adaptation to a narrow-range of agro-climatic conditions. The fourth species listed, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, is a species native to the Andes, and is cultivated only in some parts of South America and not yet commercially available in Europe (<a href="#B60" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b60"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Lucas et al., 2015<!--]--></a>). However, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> is characterized by the highest grain quality of all cultivated lupins, with an oil content similar to soybean, and is adapted to low input farming in temperate climates. The combination of these characteristics makes <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> a potentially superior alternative to the current plant-based sources of protein and oil in Europe and other regions with temperate climates.</p><p><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><i>Lupinus mutabilis</i><!--]--> Sweet is considered to be one of the lost crops of the Incas. Its seeds are characterized by a high protein and oil content (44% dw and 18% dw, respectively), which exceeds that of any other lupin species (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B70" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b70"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Pate et al., 1985<!--]--></a>). In addition, lupin seeds are practically devoid of starch, and the major carbohydrates found are oligosaccharides (mainly stachyose and raffinose) and cell wall storage polysaccharides (<a href="#B96" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b96"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Trugo et al., 2003<!--]--></a>). Most essential amino acids, lysine in particular, are also present in the seeds (<a href="#T1" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-t1"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Table 1</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>) together with a substantial amount of dietary fiber and fatty acids (<a href="#T2" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-t2"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Table 2</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>) (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B19-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b19"><!--[--><span id="B19a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Carvajal-Larenas et al., 2016<!--]--></button>). The history of this species as a subsistence crop in the Andes demonstrates its potential as a crop for low input agriculture on marginal lands. <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> shows a high adaptability to temperate and cold climates, low-fertile soils, high altitudes and harsh conditions while actively enriching the soil with nitrogen (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B24-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b24"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Cowling et al., 1998<!--]--></button>). Currently, its cultivation is mostly confined to the Andean region of South America, where its bitter seeds represent a regionally important food known as <!--[--><i>tarwi</i><!--]-->. It is an economically accessible source of good quality protein, on par with animal proteins, to a large percentage of the population (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B18-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b18"><!--[--><span id="B18a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Carvajal-Larenas, 2013<!--]--></button>).</p><div class="ArticleTable" id="T1"><div class="ArticleTable__header"><p class="ArticleTable__title">Table 1</p><!----><button data-tootip-position="top center" Lupinus as compared to Soybean (<!--[--><i>Glycine max</i><!--]-->).</p><!--]--></div><div class="ArticleTable__foot"><!--[--><p>Data are expressed in g/100 g DW (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B23-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b23"><!--[--><span id="B23a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Collins and Howell, 1957<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B47-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b47"><!--[--><span id="B47a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Hudson et al., 1983<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B73" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b73"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Prakash and Misra, 1988<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B87" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b87"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sharma et al., 2014<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B19-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b19"><!--[--><span id="B19a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Carvajal-Larenas et al., 2016<!--]--></button>) Crude fiber: insoluble residue, primarily composed of cellulose and lignin.</p><p>(tr, in traces, less than 0.1%)</p><!--]--></div></div><p>The presence of toxic alkaloids in the seeds and low yields (800–1300 kg/ha) have strongly limited the expansion of this crop (<a href="#B95" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b95"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Tapia, 2015<!--]--></a>). Selection activities by Andean farmers in the past 1,500 years of cultivation have represented the only means of domestication for <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, leading to semi-domesticated forms characterized by non-shattering pods, large seeds, multi-colored flowers, highly branched architecture and a more or less annual life cycle (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B22-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b22"><!--[--><span id="B22a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Clements et al., 2008<!--]--></button>). It played an important role as a rotation crop in Andean agriculture, but the introduction of western pulses during the Spanish conquest in the sixteenth century, led to its decline and marginalization (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B24-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b24"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Cowling et al., 1998<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). In contrast, the wide genetic diversity that characterizes this crop has enabled its adaptation to poor soils and microhabitats, preserving its cultivation in many areas where other crops cannot grow (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B18-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b18"><!--[--><span id="B18a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Carvajal-Larenas, 2013<!--]--></button>). This genetic diversity is also reflected in a broad phenotypic diversity, e.g. of seeds and flowers color (as shown in <a href="#f1" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-f1"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Figure 1</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>).</p><div class="ArticleFigure" id="f1"><div class="ArticleFigure__header"><p src="https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g001.webp" class="is-inside-mask" alt="Figure 1" loading="lazy"></picture><figcaption><!--[--><!--[--><p>Phenotypic variation in flowers and seeds of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->.</p><!--]--><!--]--></figcaption></figure></button></div><p>In recent years, efforts have been made to re-establish <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as a crop in South America, and to also adapt it to conditions in Europe (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). Numerous studies investigating the nutritional profile and potential applications of these grains have found a wide range of possible products ranging from proteins, oil, and food additives to cosmetics, medicines, and bio-pesticides. In contrast, few studies have addressed the agronomic aspect of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> cultivation. From these studies it emerges that the main obstacle to <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> cultivation is the lack of high yielding, early maturing genotypes. These results are mainly determined by an indeterminate growth habit and a lack of locally adapted genotypes, and can be overcome <!--[--><i>via</i><!--]--> breeding (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). To date <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> remains an under-studied crop, characterized by a very young and fragmented breeding history. The important role that this crop could play in the transition toward a more sustainable food production system has prompted us to review the current state of this crop. This paper summarizes past breeding achievements and sheds light on the new breeding challenges we must resolve to establish <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as a protein crop in Europe.</p><!--]--></div><div id="s2"><!--[--><h2>Origin and Distribution of the “Andean Lupin”</h2><p>The earliest archeological evidence of domesticated <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> seeds has been found in Mantaro Valley, central Peru and dates back to <!--[--><i>ca</i><!--]-->. 1800 BP. The use of RADseq in the analysis of this archeological material confirms that <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> was first domesticated not far from the Montaro Valley in the Cajamarca region (north Peru), from the wild progenitor <!--[--><i>L. piurensis</i><!--]-->. Demographic analysis suggests that <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> split from its progenitor around 2600 BP (650 BC) and suffered a domestication bottleneck and a subsequent rapid population expansion as it became cultivated across the Andes (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B8-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b8"><!--[--><span id="B8a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Atchison et al., 2016<!--]--></button>). <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> presence has been reported across the eastern side of South America, from Colombia to the North of Argentina (from 10°N to 20°S), and over a wide range of altitudes, from 1,500 to 3,800 m a.s.l. (<a href="#B53" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b53"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Jacobsen and Mujica, 2008<!--]--></a>). The crop is adapted to a temperate climate and is strongly influenced by day length. It is susceptible to low temperatures (−2°C) in the initial stages, and requires about 350–800 mm of rainfall and can grow for 240–300 days (<a href="#B52" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b52"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Jacobsen and Mujica, 2006<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B53" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b53"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Jacobsen and Mujica, 2008<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B4-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b4"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Adomas et al., 2015<!--]--></button>). Based on these requirements, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> could be cultivated in Southern Europe as a winter crop, and in Northern Europe as a summer crop. Nowadays <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> is of agricultural importance only in Ecuador, Peru, and Bolivia. Approximately 1,895 ha are cultivated in Bolivia with an average yield of 648 kg/ha, 5,974 ha in Ecuador (400 kg/ha) and 10,628 ha in Peru (1,335 kg/ha) (<a href="#B64" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b64"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Mercado et al., 2018<!--]--></a>).</p><!--]--></div><div id="s3"><!--[--><h2>Biological and Genetic Features</h2><p><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> is an annual herbaceous plant of the Fabaceae family. It is an autogamous species, with hermaphroditic flowers arranged in apical racemes, but characterized by a predominant level of allogamy. Different ranges of cross-fertilization by insects have been reported, fluctuating from 4–11% in Peru to 9.5–18.9% in Poland (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B37-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b37"><!--[--><span id="B37a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gnatowska et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). It has been observed that multiple groups of insects visit <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, suggesting that this species could be a generalist; bees of the Apidae family and bumblebees from the genus Xylocopa are the main visitors in native environments, while bumblebees from the genus Bombus are more common in Europe (<a href="#B66" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b66"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Ochoa-Zavala et al., 2016<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B7-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b7"><!--[--><span id="B7a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Arnold et al., 2014<!--]--></button>). The isolation of different genotypes is thus indispensable in breeding programs, as much as the careful wrapping of emasculated flowers in intraspecific hybridization (<a href="#B98" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b98"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Von Baer, 2011<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B4-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b4"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Adomas et al., 2015<!--]--></button>). Phylogenetic analysis places <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> (2n = 48) within the Andean clade of Western New World species of the genus Lupinus. This genus includes almost 300 species, grouped by their different centers of origin into Old World (Mediterranean) and New World (American) subgenera. To date, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> is the only cultivated species from the New World group (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B39-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b39"><!--[--><span id="B39a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gresta et al., 2017<!--]--></button>). Notably, the Andean clade to which it belongs is characterized by the highest speciation rate within the genus (<a href="#B49" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b49"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Hughes and Eastwood, 2006<!--]--></a>). The species in this clade belong to a paleoploid group of plants with basic chromosome number x = 6 (<a href="#B65" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b65"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Naganowska et al., 2003<!--]--></a>). Events of allo- and autopolyploidization, together with other chromosomal rearrangements, during the evolution of this species might have led to duplication/or triplication of genome regions, as observed in the Old World species <!--[--><i>Lupinus angustifolius</i><!--]--> (<a href="#B56" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b56"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Kroc et al., 2014<!--]--></a>).</p><!--]--></div><div id="s4"><!--[--><h2>The Unexploited Potential of <!--[--><i>L. Mutabilis</i><!--]-->, an Under-Studied Crop</h2><p><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> appears to be a valid alternative to soybeans for satisfying plant protein requirements in Europe. Like soybean, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> seeds are rich in proteins as well as in oil. They can find applications as food and feed, but also as raw materials for the production of bio-based products. On the other hand, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> cultivation tolerates better cold climates and can therefore contribute to the production and diversification of sustainable European sources of proteins and oil.</p><p>However, despite the clear potential, research on <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> has been limited. As is often the case for under-utilized crops, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> has been long neglected by research and industry due to its limited economic importance on the global market. A recent domestication and a breeding history fragmented in time and space have also contributed to this neglect, resulting in a lack of genetic improvement and inferior yield. In the Andes, <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> germplasm collection and breeding programs started only in the 1970s and have so far relied on participatory approaches with farmers for the selection of local ecotypes (<a href="#T3" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-t3"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Table 3</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). The selection of genotypes with better yields mainly relies on the geographical distribution and vegetative cycle of the ecotypes and it has rarely resulted in the registration of cultivars (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B32-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b32"><!--[--><span id="B32a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Fries and Tapia, 2007<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B71" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b71"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Peralta et al., 2012<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B97" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b97"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Vicente Rojas, 2016<!--]--></a>). In Europe, researchers began working on the selection of sweet lines in the 1920s, but it was only in the 1970s, when the nutritional value of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> seeds became well known, that the interest for this crop arose. The difficult accessibility of germplasm from the Andean area was overcome in Europe with a large use of induced mutations and intraspecific crossing of mutants. Preliminary field trials of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> in Europe reported large differences in seed yields, from 0.5 to 6.5 t/ha depending on years and location (<a href="#B63" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b63"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Masefield, 1976<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B78" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b78"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Romer and Jahn-Deesbach, 1992<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B100" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b100"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Weissmann and Weissmann, 1992<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B80" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b80"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Rubenschuh, 1997<!--]--></a>). In 1993, the first European project aimed at evaluating the “Adaptation of <!--[--><i>Lupinus mutabilis</i><!--]--> to European soil and climate conditions” was funded. Field trials reported very low seed yields (1.1 t/ha) and pointed out the need of breeding for a better plant architecture and early maturity (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). Many years of mutation experiments in Poland have resulted in improvement in yield and sweetness and in the selection of determinate lines for research purposes, but not yet in the establishment or registration of new varieties (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B33-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b33"><!--[--><span id="B33a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek, 2010<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B35-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b35"><!--[--><span id="B35a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek et al., 2017<!--]--></button>) (<a href="#T3" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-t3"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Table 3</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). Australia has also shown interest in <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> and multiple projects to evaluate its potential for southern Australia were funded. Of particular relevance in their work was the selection of male sterile lines, used to introduce early vigor, anthracnose resistance, tolerance to brown spot and resistance to cucumber mosaic virus in <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> (<a href="#B92" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b92"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sweetingham et al., 2006<!--]--></a>) (<a href="#T3" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-t3"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Table 3</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). The ongoing development of recombinant inbred lines (RIL) population at the University of Western Australia is mentioned in the literature and could be exploited for mapping QTLs, however little information is available about its existence and state (J. C. Clements and M. N. Nelson, unpubl. data in <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B10-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b10"><!--[--><span id="B10a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Berger et al., 2013<!--]--></button>).</p><div class="ArticleTable" id="T3"><div class="ArticleTable__header"><p class="ArticleTable__title">Table 3</p><!----><button data-tootip-position="top center" class="ButtonIcon ButtonIcon--black ButtonIcon--medium" aria-label="Expand Table" data-event="articleTable-button-openLightbox"><!--[--><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 48 48" fill="currentColor" width="24px" style="vertical-align:middle;display:inline-block;height:auto;" color="black"><path d="M28 10V6h14v14h-4v-7.171L27.414 23.413l-2.828-2.828L35.172 10H28ZM6 28h4v7.172l10.586-10.586 2.828 2.828L12.828 38H20v4H6V28Z"></path></svg><!--]--></button></div><div class="ArticleTable__content"><table><!--[--><thead><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><th valign="top">Area of Selection</th><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><th valign="top">Line</th><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><th valign="top">Characteristics</th><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><th valign="top">Reference</th><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></thead><tbody><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><b>Chile</b><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Inti*</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Stable cultivar with 0.0075% alkaloid content in seeds, but low yield and long vegetation period.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B41-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b41"><!--[--><span id="B41a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gross et al., 1988<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B98" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b98"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Von Baer, 2011<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><b>Bolivia</b><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Chumpi, TarwiNawi</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Ecotypes grown in Potosi, characterized by dark brown seeds.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B97" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b97"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Vicente Rojas, 2016<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Tolarapa,Dulce</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Ecotypes grown in the area of Cochabamba.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B40-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b40"><!--[--><span id="B40a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gross and Baer, 1981<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Carabuco*</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Variety inscribed in the National Register of seeds. Characterized by early maturing and white seeds with a cuboid flat shape.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B97" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b97"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Vicente Rojas, 2016<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><b>Ecuador</b><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">I-450 Andino*I-451 Guaranguito*</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Early maturing genotypes (6 months), uniform white seeds and higher yield (1370 kg/ha on average). Susceptible to anthracnose.Registered by INIAP.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B71" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b71"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Peralta et al., 2012<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">ECU-2700, ECU-2658</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Genotypes selected for resistance to anthracnose and high yield (1445 kg/ha on average).</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B42-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b42"><!--[--><span id="B42a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Guaytarilla and Falconi, 2014<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><b>Poland</b><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">KW-1</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Completely determinate mutant, with no lateral branches. Characterized by tall growth, liability to lodge and low seed production.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B77" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b77"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Römer, 1994<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td and lower alkaloid content obtained combining intraspecific crosses with induced mutation.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B85" class="ArticleReference" Rybinski, 2001<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B33-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b33"><!--[--><span id="B33a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek, 2010<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td P28725</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Advanced low alkaloid, breeding lines to assess adaptation of the species to eastern states.</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Clements et al., 2008<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">P27033</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Male sterile line</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B92" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b92"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sweetingham et al., 2006<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">P25954</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Restorer line</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B92" class="ArticleReference" valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">P26961</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Early line</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B2-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b2"><!--[--><span id="B2a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Adhikari et al., 2012<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">P27808</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Mid-season line</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Adhikari et al., 2012<!--]--></button>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><tr><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><b>Russia</b><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">KVIR2381</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">Russian breeding line used in crosses to introduce tolerance to brown spot and resistance to cucumber mosaic virus (CMV).</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><td valign="top">(<a href="#B92" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b92"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sweetingham et al., 2006<!--]--></a>)</td><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tr><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--></tbody><!--]--></table></div><div class="ArticleTable__description"><!--[--><p>A list of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> lines involved in breeding research.</p><!--]--></div><div class="ArticleTable__foot"><!--[--><p>*L. mutabilis lines that developed into cultivars.</p><!--]--></div></div><p>In Europe, the urgent need to provide alternative protein sources and recover marginal land has contributed to revive the interest in <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->. Recently, a new program investigating <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> cropping in marginal lands for enhanced bio economy has been funded under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 program (<!--[--><a href="http://www.libbio.net/">www.libbio.net</a><!--]-->). The possibility of cultivating <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as a summer crop in North-central Europe and winter crop in the Mediterranean area is being investigated, along with the development of pre-industrial processing and the assessment of its socio-economic and environmental impact.</p><!--]--></div><div id="s5"><!--[--><h2>Establishing <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as a Protein Crop in EUROPE: The Breeding Challenges</h2><!--[--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><h3>Adaptation to European Environment</h3><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>The environmental differences between the native environment of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> and other cultivation areas around the world, such as europe, represent one of the barriers to the expansion of this crop. In temperate climatic conditions <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> cultivation is characterized by a long period of maturation and uneven maturation of the pod, blossom drop, and shattering of early stage pods (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B46-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b46"><!--[--><span id="B46a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Hardy et al., 1998<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B93" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b93"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Swiecicki and Nawrot, 2004<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B34-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b34"><!--[--><span id="B34a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek et al., 2007<!--]--></button>). Due to low resistance to frost during the first growth stage, sowing is limited to autumn in mediterranean environments and to spring in northern countries. In both cases, the crop will reach flowering towards the beginning of the dry season. Dry conditions can accelerate maturation, but considerably affect the biomass yield, pod set and consequently, the seed yield (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B45-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b45"><!--[--><span id="B45a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Hardy et al., 1997<!--]--></button>). Hence, it is crucial to generate early maturing genotypes with increased drought tolerance and consistent yield performance (yield stability). Previous work has pointed out that vernalization has no effect on early and mid-season genotypes of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, but can reduce the flowering time of late season genotypes such as Inti. It was observed that a vernalization period of 2–4 weeks at 6°C can shorten flowering time of four weeks in Inti, reducing the gap between early- and late-flowering lines to only 3 weeks (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B2-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b2"><!--[--><span id="B2a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Adhikari et al., 2012<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>Research on drought stress in <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> has uncovered the existence of different drought tolerance strategies across genotypes, either <!--[--><i>via</i><!--]--> stomatal adjustments or through the accumulation of osmoprotectants. Some traits, like stomatal conductance and water potential, appear to decrease uniformly among all accessions while other traits such as membrane ion leakage or accumulation of proline and soluble sugars show particular trends depending on the genotype. This might indicate the ability of some <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> genotypes to adapt their cell membrane during periods of water stress, as an alternative strategy to stomatal adjustment (<a href="#B59" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b59"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Lizarazo et al., 2010<!--]--></a>). Therefore, both stomatal conductance and membrane ion leakage can prove useful in the selection of drought resistant cultivars.</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>Response to photoperiod is another important factor for determining adaptation to different locations. Reports on photoperiodic sensitivity in <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> are contrasting. Hackbarth reported <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> as neutral to day length, while Jacobsen and Mujica affirm that in the Andean region <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> accelerates grain filling when the day length is short (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B43-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b43"><!--[--><span id="B43a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Hackbarth, 1961<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B53" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b53"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Jacobsen and Mujica, 2008<!--]--></a>).Given the latter, adaptation at higher latitudes should be based on the selection of lines less sensitive to day length effects on grain filling. A more exhaustive understanding of sensitivity to day length through characterization of germplasm collections and knowledge about its genetic basis would enable the generation of genotypes for high latitudes with little or no sensitivity (<a href="#B53" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b53"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Jacobsen and Mujica, 2008<!--]--></a>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><h3>Growth Habit: Toward a Semi-Determinate Type</h3><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>Indeterminate growth habit and sympodial branching pattern have been identified as the main factors limiting yield of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> in European field trials (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>). In <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> the vegetative development begins with the production of a main stem bearing a terminal inflorescence and continues with the production of successive orders of branches throughout the entire growing season (that can be from 0 to 52 branches), as long as growing conditions are favorable (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B45-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b45"><!--[--><span id="B45a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Hardy et al., 1997<!--]--></button>) (<a href="#f2" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-f2"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Figure 2A</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). This growth habit leads to an overlap of vegetative and reproductive phases, characterized in this species by a preferential partitioning of nutrients to vegetative growth. As a result, the possibility of uniform maturation is hindered and reproductive growth is constantly delayed, often so far as to coincide with late-season drought, thus further reducing productivity. Furthermore, only the racemes of the main stem and first order branches are highly productive, while the production becomes progressively weaker on the other order of branches (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B3-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b3"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Adhikari et al., 2001<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><div class="ArticleFigure" id="f2"><div class="ArticleFigure__header"><p class="ArticleFigure__title">Figure 2</p><button data-tootip-position="top center" class="ButtonIcon ButtonIcon--black class="FrontiersImage"><picture class="FrontiersImage"><!--[--><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=340&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 1x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=680&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 2x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1020&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 3x" media="(max-width: 563px)"><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=612&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 1x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1224&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 2x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1836&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 3x" media="(max-width: 768px)"><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=654&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 1x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1308&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 2x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1962&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 3x" media="(max-width: 1280px)"><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=631&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 1x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1262&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 2x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1893&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 3x" media="(max-width: 1441px)"><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=716&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 1x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=1432&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 2x, https://www.frontiersin.org/api/ipx/w=2148&amp;f=webp/https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp 3x" media><source srcset="https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp" media><!--]--><img src="https://www.frontiersin.org/files/Articles/485490/xml-images/fpls-10-01385-g002.webp" class="is-inside-mask" alt="Figure 2" loading="lazy"></picture><figcaption><!--[--><!--[--><p>Different growth forms of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->.</p><!--]--><!--]--></figcaption></figure></button></div><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>It has long been proposed that the development of determinate lines could guarantee a more stable seed yield by providing an earlier and more uniform maturation (<a href="#B50" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b50"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Huyghe, 1998<!--]--></a>). Determinate lupin cultivars have been obtained in <!--[--><i>L. albus, L. luteus, and L. angustifolius</i><!--]--> mainly through selection of spontaneous or induced mutants. Vavilov’s homologous order of restricted branching (<!--[--><i>rb</i><!--]-->) was selected independently in these species with a different mode of inheritance, a different number of alleles in the <!--[--><i>rb</i><!--]--> locus and somewhat differentiated expression in the respective species (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B38-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b38"><!--[--><span id="B38a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gorynowicz et al., 2014<!--]--></button>). Similarly, a completely determinate line of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> was found upon induced mutation with EMS. This mono-stem determinate mutant—<!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> KW 1—did not produce lateral branches, matured early and was characterized by tall stems (<a href="#B85" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b85"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sawicka, 1993<!--]--></a>) (<a href="#f2" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-f2"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Figure 2C</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). The inheritance of the determinate character was found to be monogenic recessive (<a href="#B77" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b77"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Römer, 1994<!--]--></a>). Unfortunately, determinate plants were found to lodge and were not able to compensate for stress during main stem flowering because of increased pod set on the branches (<a href="#B76" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b76"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Romer, 1995<!--]--></a>). Semi-determinate types with only one or two orders of lateral branches up to the top of the plant thus seem preferable from an agronomic point of view (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B16-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b16"><!--[--><span id="B16a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Caligari et al., 2000<!--]--></button>) (<a href="#f2" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-f2"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Figure 2B</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). Ongoing research in Poland has focused on crossing the KW-1 mutant with early maturing mutants characterized by a reduced number of side branches for the selection of determinate form for research purposes (<a href="#B84" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b84"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sawicka-Sienkiewicz and Kadlubiec, 2001<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B83" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b83"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Sawicka-Sienkiewicz et al., 2005<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B34-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b34"><!--[--><span id="B34a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek et al., 2007<!--]--></button>). Determinate forms distinguished by medium-tall stems without lateral branches, resistant to lodgings and with early generative growth have been obtained (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B34-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b34"><!--[--><span id="B34a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek et al., 2007<!--]--></button>). Indeterminate forms appear to have a higher mass of stems and plant aerial parts and a lower share of seeds in the yield structure, and may therefore be more suitable for biomass production (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B36-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b36"><!--[--><span id="B36a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gas, 2014<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B4-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b4"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Adomas et al., 2015<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><h3>Understanding the Mechanisms Regulating Alkaloid Content</h3><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>Food and feed industries have set the strict threshold of 0.02% (DM weight) alkaloid content in lupin seeds (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B24-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b24"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Cowling et al., 1998<!--]--></button>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B31-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b31"><!--[--><span id="B31a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Frick et al., 2017<!--]--></button>). Quinolizidine Alkaloids (QAs) are typically synthesized by lupin species and are mainly known for causing bitter taste and anticholinergic toxicity when present in the grains. However, QAs also play an important role in the mechanism of defense against pathogens and predators, have allelopathic functions (<a href="#B104" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b104"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Wink, 1993<!--]--></a>) and constitute nitrogen reserves for the plant (<a href="#B107" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b107"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Wink and Witte, 1985<!--]--></a>). They are biosynthesized from L-lysine in green tissues of the plant, transported <!--[--><i>via</i><!--]--> phloem and stored in all the organs of the plant, especially seeds. The content and composition of QAs depend on many factors, including genotype, biotic/abiotic stresses and pedoclimatic conditions. Each lupin species is characterized by a different alkaloid profile, known as an alkaloid-fingerprint, which fluctuates among the different organs of the plant, expressing a lower diversity and concentration in leaves than in seeds (<a href="#B105" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b105"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Wink et al., 1995<!--]--></a>; <button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B13-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b13"><!--[--><span id="B13a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Boschin and Resta, 2013<!--]--></button>). Although the chemistry of Quinolizidine alkaloids has been extensively studied leading to the identification of more than 170 structures (<a href="#B104" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b104"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Wink, 1993<!--]--></a>), their biosynthetic pathway is only partially elucidated and information on the genes and enzymes involved remains limited (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B31-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b31"><!--[--><span id="B31a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Frick et al., 2017<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>The breeding of sweet lines of <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> has been mainly based on the selection of natural and induced mutants, mostly in Chile, Poland and Australia. The first stable “sweet” variety, Inti, was bred in Chile in 1980. It was characterized by an alkaloid content of 0.0075% with no reported detrimental effect on the protein (51%) or oil (16%) content, but low yield and long vegetation period hindered its adoption in different places (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B41-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b41"><!--[--><span id="B41a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Gross et al., 1988<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B98" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b98"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Von Baer, 2011<!--]--></a>). Yet, the inheritance of the trait was recessive and of polygenic nature, such that only 12% of the F2 plants had low alkaloid content (Von Baer and Von Baer). These characteristics require major efforts to maintain the purity of mother’s lines and to prevent the risk of progressive re-bittering due to cross-pollination in regions where lupin grows in the wild (<a href="#B81" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b81"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Santana and Empis, 2001<!--]--></a>; <a href="#B98" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b98"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Von Baer, 2011<!--]--></a>). In 1984, seed treatments with ethyl methanesulfonate led to the identification of the recessive allele <!--[--><i>mutal</i><!--]--> of the gene <!--[--><i>Mutal</i><!--]-->. When homozygous, the allele <!--[--><i>mutal</i><!--]--> was found to reduce the alkaloid level to 0.2–0.3% of seeds DM, giving rise to plants organoleptically sweet both in their seeds and vegetative parts (<a href="#B102" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b102"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Williams et al., 1984<!--]--></a>). It has been suggested that along the reselection process additional minor alleles were recombined at several loci to lower alkaloid levels (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B22-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b22"><!--[--><span id="B22a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Clements et al., 2008<!--]--></button>). At present, none of the mutations found has led to complete suppression of alkaloids. The reduction in total alkaloids is mainly due to a reduced percentage of sparteine and lupanine, the two most toxic QAs to humans (<a href="#B102" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b102"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Williams et al., 1984<!--]--></a>). The result of the work done in Chile in the last 40 years is the acquisition of a new variety, PINTA (Inti x SCG9) which combines low content of alkaloids, high content of protein and oil, and large seeds (<a href="#B98" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b98"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Von Baer, 2011<!--]--></a>). In Poland, post-mutagen treated material has been widely screened using iodine test to select 13 lines that don’t exceed 0.1% of alkaloid content in the seeds. These genotypes can be very useful in breeding programs, particularly to derive homozygous lines. In addition to facilitating the development of stables sweet varieties these homozygous lines can also be used to study the inheritance of alkaloid content in seeds (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B35-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b35"><!--[--><span id="B35a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Galek et al., 2017<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>A major drawback of reducing alkaloids is the increased sensitivity of plants to pests and diseases. Future work should therefore target the development of bitter/sweet lines, with sufficient level of alkaloids in the vegetative tissues to deter pathogens, but low levels in the seeds (<a href="#B103" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b103"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Wink, 1990<!--]--></a>). To use this strategy fundamental knowledge on how to target the transporters involved in the translocation of QAs from source tissues to seeds is required. Candidate transporters may include plasma membrane importers in cells of reproductive tissue, and vacuolar membrane importers in cells of both aerial and reproductive tissues, as alkaloids are often sequestered within vacuoles to avoid toxic effects within tissues (<a href="#B110" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b110"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Yazaki et al., 2008<!--]--></a>). To our knowledge, there are no studies yet investigating these mechanisms.</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><h3>Seed Color, a Matter of Acceptance</h3><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>To further develop the market for <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, it is essential to take into account consumer preferences. When whole lupin beans are marketed as food, seed coat color becomes a decisive trait for the acceptance of a cultivar. As for <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]-->, white color is the most attractive for consumers. The phenotypical diversity in seed shape and seed coat color observed in this species appears to be larger than that in all the other lupins (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>). Seed characteristics with large diversity include shape (from lenticulate to spherical), primary seed color, secondary seed color and its pattern distribution (<a href="#f1" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-f1"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><b>Figure 1</b><!--]--><!--]--></a>). The color can vary from pearly white to solid black, and include beige/yellow, brown, dark brown and intermediate colors, like brownish green and greyish colors. Most seeds have a secondary color distribution in darker tones of the primary color. The secondary color distribution also varies between a large range of patterns, such as moustache, eyebrow, crescent, marbled, or spotted which can be expressed singularly or in combination (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B28-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b28"><!--[--><span id="B28a" class="ArticleReference__scrollTarget" aria-hidden="true"></span><!--]--><!--[-->Falconí, 2012<!--]--></button>; <a href="#B95" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b95"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Tapia, 2015<!--]--></a>). The variability in seed coat color may reflect the genetic pressure <!--[--><i>L. mutabilis</i><!--]--> was subjected to during its domestication, but very little is known about the genetic mechanism behind this trait. Some authors try to explain this variation hypothesizing the concerted effect of different alleles in the control of different colors and at different regions of the seed coat. That is, having different genes controlling the primary color, the secondary color, the color of the hilum and/or its adjacent region and the different patterns of distribution of secondary colors (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>). Another possible explanation for the existence of such diversity in seed color and patterns may be the presence of transposable elements, as observed in other crops (<a href="#B57" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b57"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Li et al., 2012<!--]--></a>). There appears to be a connection between seed color and flower color. Darker seeds lead to darker flowers, suggesting that the white color behaves as a recessive character (<button type="button" color="Blue40" id="B12-button" class="ArticleReference" data-event="articleReference-a-b12"><!--[--><!--]--><!--[-->Blanco-Galdos, 1982<!--]--></button>).</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><p>The complexity in seed color represents a great challenge for breeders to select pure lines with uniform and heritable colors and patterns, and in particular to combine locus for high yield and white color. Still, pearly white is in 95% of the cases the most common color found in the cultivars sampled for germplasm collections of the Andean regions.</p><!--]--><!--[--><!--]--><!--]--><!--[--><!--[--><!--]--><!--[--><h3>Identification of
